- N–B: visible line up the slope.

Image name: Tu_task2_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gpt-5
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 10
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
- Target nodes: E (black) and I (black). We must remove white nodes only.
- List neighbors and key connections:
  - E connects to: B, P, F, L, M.
  - I connects to: H, C, B, P, J.
- Any E–I path must enter I through one of {H, C, B, P, J} and leave E through one of {B, P, F, L, M}.
- Observe edge B–I and B–E: there is a direct path E–B–I using only node B. Thus, any cut must remove B or otherwise block E–B or B–I; only node-removal option is to remove B.
- If B is removed, remaining possible routes from E to I must use P, F, L, or M from E and then reach one of {H, C, P, J} adjacent to I.
  - From E via P: P connects to O, J, D (through J), and to I directly. With B removed, E–P–I is still a path, so we must also remove P.
- Check if {B, P} suffices:
  - Routes via F: From E–F… From F you can go to G and X (and K), and to J via edge? No, F is not connected to J or H or C or I except through the long line F–E–P–…; also there’s an edge from I to J only. There is a crossing near F–P line, but F is not connected to P except through E. From F’s cluster (G, X, K) there is no path to I’s neighbors {H, C, J} or to I itself. So F route is blocked.
  - Routes via L or M: L–M–triangle to E only; M connects to J? Yes, J connects to M via a line; verify: In the image, J has edges to I, P, D, and M. Yes, there is J–M. Then E–M–J–I would be a path. But with P removed and B removed, E–M–J–I still exists. So we must ensure this is blocked. However, note E also connects directly to M; we already use M. Since we cannot remove J or M in our current set, {B, P} is not enough.
  - So we need to block the J route as well. We can remove J or M or L. Removing M suffices to break E–M–J–I and E–L–M–J–I. Removing J also works.

- Try a smaller set than three:
  - Could {B, J} work without removing P? No, E–P–I would remain.
  - Could {P, J} work without removing B? No, E–B–I remains.
  - Therefore at least three nodes are necessary: one to block the B route, one to block the P route, and one to block the J/M/L route.

- Choose a minimal 3-node cut set:
  - {B, P, J} disconnects all:
    - Path via B: removed.
    - Path via P: removed.
    - Path via J through M/L: removed since J is removed; without J, L/M cannot reach I.
    - Any remaining route from E via F or its cluster cannot reach I’s neighborhood.
    - From E via L or M directly to anything else: without J, they connect only back to E or each other.
    - From E via O through P? E is not connected to O directly; only via P which is removed.
    - From E via B alternative through C/H? With B removed, E has no connection upward to C/H.

Therefore, the minimum is 3, and one valid cut set is [B, P, J].

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, P, J]

Image name: POGBa_task2_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gpt-5
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 10
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
- Target nodes are P and N.
- List all connections toward P: P connects to I, D, O, and J (vertical via J) — actually J connects to P by vertical edge.
- List all connections toward N: N connects to C (vertical), B (horizontal via bottom L-shape), and O (horizontal).
- Trace all P–N paths:
  1) P–O–N.
  2) P–O–C–N.
  3) P–I–C–N.
  4) P–I–H–B–N (via B–N bottom edge).
  5) P–I–H–B–C–N (B–C–N).
  6) P–J–I–… (joins paths through I).
  7) P–D–(back through J square to P only) — D only connects to P and J box; from D you can go to J then I, so again via I.
  Thus, every path from P to N must pass through at least one of the three nodes {O, C, I} or through B for the route to N via B. Check:
  - From P to N via O requires O.
  - Via C requires C (either P–I–C–N or P–O–C–N).
  - Via B requires both I and B (P–I–H–B–N). There is no edge from P to H or B directly; D/J routes also funnel to I before reaching H/B.
- Therefore, to block all routes, removing {O, C} is sufficient? Check:
  - Remove O and C: 
    • Path P–O–N is cut (O removed).
    • Paths through C (P–I–C–N and P–O–C–N) are cut (C removed).
    • Remaining possibility is P–I–H–B–N which does not use O or C; this still exists. So {O, C} is not enough.
- What about {O, I}?
  - Cuts P–O–N (O removed).
  - Cuts any path that needs I (P–I–C–N, P–I–H–B–N, and routes via J or D which go to I). 
  - Remaining possible path P–O–C–N would need O (removed). So all paths blocked.
- Can a single node suffice?
  - Remove O: still P–I–C–N and P–I–H–B–N exist.
  - Remove I: still P–O–N and P–O–C–N exist.
  - Remove C: still P–O–N and P–I–H–B–N exist.
  - Remove B: still P–O–N and P–I–C–N exist.
  Hence no single-node cut works.
- Is there any 2-node cut smaller than {O, I}? Check other pairs:
  - {O, B}: Leaves P–I–C–N.
  - {I, C}: Leaves P–O–N.
  - {C, B}: Leaves P–O–N.
  - {I, B}: Leaves P–O–N.
  Thus {O, I} (or symmetrically {O, B}? No) The only effective size-2 options are those that remove O and also block the I-route; that means {O, I}. Removing O and B still allows P–I–C–N, so invalid. Removing O and C blocks I–C but still I–H–B–N, so invalid. Therefore the minimal cut is size 2 with the unique pair {O, I}.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [O, I]

Image name: POGBa_task2_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gpt-5
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 10
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:

Target nodes: G and I (black).

List all neighbors and main routes between G and I.
- G connects to: X, H, F, and via top-left outer frame to J (through long outer path G–H–I–J is not needed since J links to P–I; but check direct neighbors first).
- Immediate edges around the center: G–H–I is a direct 2-edge path via H. Also verticals: H–B and I–C allow paths G–H–B–C–I. Through X/F/E/B/C there are more: G–X–F–E–B–C–I. There is also the right side through P: G–H–I–P–J loop doesn’t help; but G can go G–H–I directly unless we cut somewhere before I.

To disconnect G from I, any path from left block to right block must pass through one of the three nodes adjacent to I’s side from the left: H, C, or P (since I’s neighbors are H, C, and P). If all three are removed, I is isolated from the left. Conversely, removing just one or two leaves a remaining route (examples: if H is removed, G→…→B→C→I works; if C is removed, G→H→I works; if P is removed, G→H→I still works). Therefore at least two removals are insufficient because with any pair removed, at least one of {H, C, P} remains that still has a path from G.

We must also ensure G can reach each of H, C, P without passing through the others:
- To H: direct edge G–H.
- To C: G→H→B→C or G→X→F→E→B→C, so reachable without P.
- To P: G→H→I→P would use I, not allowed as an intermediate? Using I would already reach I; but from G to P without touching I: path G→H→B→C→O→P exists via the bottom row. Hence P is reachable from G independently.

Thus every G–I path must enter I through one of H, C, or P, and G has routes to each of these that avoid the others, so the minimum vertex cut equals 3: {H, C, P}.

Verification:
- Remove H, C, and P. Then I has no remaining neighbors ⇒ all paths from G to I are broken.
- No 1- or 2-node set works as argued above since at least one of H, C, P would remain and is connected to G, giving a path to I.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [H, C, P]

- J connects to I, P, M, and D. (Edges visible: I–J, P–J, M–J, D–J.)

4) Enumerate all distinct path groups from L to J:
- Through M: L–M–J (direct via M).
- Through E then M: L–E–M–J.
- Through E then P: L–E–P–J.
- Through E then I: L–E–P–I–J or L–E–B–I–J (since E–B vertical, B–I via C/B/H lines? There is a direct I–B edge visible via the central link B–I; yes.)
- Through E then F side: L–E–P–F–…–J? F connects to J only via P–J edge; F–J is not an edge. So any F route still funnels back to P then J.

Thus, every path from L to J must pass through at least one of the nodes in {M, P, I}. Explanation:
- If a path uses M, it includes M.
- If it avoids M, the only edges from the L-side subgraph (triangle L–E–M plus E’s connections) toward J’s region are via E–P and E–B–I/ E–P–I. From E to J without M requires going either to P or to I (since E connects to P and B; B connects to I; I connects to J; P connects to J). So any M-avoiding path must include P or I.

5) Minimality check:
- Removing a single node:
  - Remove M: paths L–E–P–J and L–E–P–I–J remain.
  - Remove P: paths L–M–J and L–E–B–I–J remain.
  - Remove I: paths L–M–J and L–E–P–J remain.
Therefore, any single node is insufficient.

6) Try size-2 cut sets:
- Remove {M, P}: Remaining route L–E–B–I–J still exists via I, so not sufficient.
- Remove {M, I}: Then all remaining connections from E go to P, but P–J would be needed; with I removed, could there be a bypass not using P? From E you can go to B, but from B to J without I there is no edge (B connects to C, O, N, I, E; none go to J). From E to P to J would be blocked if P is present? Wait J is still connected to P; but we removed I, not P, so path L–E–P–J exists. Thus {M, I} is not sufficient.
- Remove {P, I}: Then any M-avoiding path is gone, and direct M path L–M–J remains unless M is also removed. So with {P, I} removed, only possible route is via M, but L–M–J is available; hence not sufficient.

Hence we need at least 3 nodes.

7) Size-3 cut:
- Remove {M, P, I}: 
  - Paths through M are broken.
  - M-avoiding paths must go via P or I; both are removed.
  - From E to B to … cannot reach J because I is removed and there is no alternate connection from B’s side to J.
Therefore all L–J paths are severed.

No smaller cut works as shown in step 5 and 6.

  - Could there still be a path via I? From E to I requires E–B–I or E–P–I. Cutting (E, B) blocks E–B–I; and with (J, P) removed, E–P–I–J is impossible since E–P exists but P–J is cut; E–P–I–… to J would need I–J, but still needs P–J? No, I–J is direct; however E–P–I uses (P, I) which exists; then I–J gives a path E–P–I–J. So to block I-route we must either cut (P, I) or (I, J) or prevent reaching P. We still have E–P edge intact, so we must address I.
- Adjust cut set: choose (I, J) instead of (J, P) to eliminate all I-based entries while leaving P-based ones; but then E–P–J remains.
- Minimal approach: cut the three “gate” edges into J that are actually reachable after cheap E-side cuts.
  - If we cut (E, M) and (E, F), the only remaining ways from E to J are via P–J and via B–I–J (and possibly via P–I–J).
  - Cut a single edge (I, J) to block all I-based paths.
  - Cut (P, J) to block the P/D route.
- Verify remaining possibilities: With edges removed [(E, M), (E, F), (I, J), (P, J)], there is no way from E to reach J:
  - Via M: blocked at (E, M).
  - Via F: blocked at (E, F).
  - Via P: blocked at (P, J) (and D depends on P–J as well).
  - Via I: blocked at (I, J).
  - No other edge connects to J.

Can we do it with only three edges? Try removing edges into J only: need to cover {J–M, J–F, J–P, J–I}. At least four edges required unless some are made unreachable by a single upstream cut. One upstream cut like (E, F) makes J–F irrelevant, but still have J–M, J–P, J–I paths; that’s three more, totaling four. Any single upstream cut cannot simultaneously block more than one of those disjoint entrances, so fewer than four edges isn’t enough.

Therefore the minimal cut size is 4.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(E, M), (E, F), (I, J), (P, J)]

- Therefore every H–O path must pass through at least one of the two nodes B or I.

Verify minimality:
- Removing only B still leaves path H–I–P–O (or H–I–C–O). So one node is insufficient.
- Removing only I still leaves path H–B–C–O (or H–B–P–O, H–B–N–O). Thus one node is insufficient.
- Removing both B and I:
  - Paths via B are cut.
  - Paths via I are cut.
  - Any route via G must go to B or I (G has no other connection to the right), both removed, so blocked.
  - No other direct edge from H to any node that reaches O without using B or I exists.
Hence H and O become disconnected.

  - So {I,B} is not enough.

- If we remove G and B:
  - Remaining neighbor to H is I. Is there a path from E to I avoiding G and B? Yes: E→M→(frame top)→J→P→I or E→C→I if C reachable: E→B is removed, so not via C; but outer frame route via J→P→I works. So not enough.

- H would have no remaining neighbor (since H’s neighbors are G, I, B). That disconnects immediately, regardless of other routes.
- Verify minimality: Can two nodes ever suffice?

- Remove {M, P}: J would still connect via I (J–I–H/B/…); F can reach I via G–H–I or via E–B–C–I. So path remains.
- Remove {I, P}: J would still connect via M (J–M–E–F or through E–…); since E connects directly to F, that path exists. So any pair among {M, I, P} fails.

Therefore we need at least three nodes, and removing {M, I, P} severs all links from J to the rest of the graph:
- With M removed, edges J–M and E–M are gone.
- With I removed, edges J–I and all routes through I are gone.
- With P removed, edges J–P and D–P, O–P are gone; D becomes a dead end.
- After removing all three, J has no remaining neighbor connected to the graph (all its neighbors M, I, P are removed), so no F→J path can exist.

Minimality: Since any two-node subset leaves at least one neighbor of J (among M, I, P) intact, a path remains via that neighbor. Thus, 3 is the minimum.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, I, P]

Now enumerate all B→M paths:
1) B → E → M.
2) B → E → L → M.
3) B → H → I → J → M.
4) B → H → I → P → J → M.
5) B → H → I → P → D → J → M (triangle J–P–D).
6) B → E → I → J → M (since E connects to I).
7) B → E → I → P → J → M.
8) B → C → I → J → M (since C–I).
9) B → C → O → P → J → M (O–P).
10) B → N → O → P → J → M (N–O).
11) B → C → N → Y → O …? Y connects only to N and O, so C→N→Y→O→P→J→M is another.

Observe chokepoints toward M’s side:
- Any path to M must enter the set {E, L, J} right before M, because M’s neighbors are exactly E, L, J.
- From B’s side, reaching L requires going through E (edge L–E). There is no other route to L. So all paths to M via L pass through E first.
- Therefore every B→M path must pass through either E or J right before M (L option is subsumed by E).
- Can a path reach J without using E? Yes, via H→I→J or via C/N/O→P→J, etc.
- Can a path reach E without using J? Yes: B→E→M or B→E→L→M or E→I→… but to M from E can go directly E→M.

Hence, to disconnect, we must block both gateways {E} and {J}. Removing only one leaves paths through the other.

Verify cut set {E, J}:
- Remove E: Paths 1,2,6,7 are broken; also routes that try to go through L are blocked.
- Remove J: Paths 3,4,5,7,8,9,10,11 are broken (anything entering J). With both removed, there is no remaining neighbor of M to reach from B, since L is unreachable without E.

Minimality:
- A single node cannot suffice: If remove E only, path B→H→I→J→M remains. If remove J only, path B→E→M remains. No other single node blocks both families simultaneously.

Therefore the minimum cut is 2 nodes.

- It blocks the direct route via M–J.
- It blocks every route that leaves via E (since both exits from E are cut).

  - If (M, J) and (E, B) are removed, L–E–P–J remains.

- From G there are edges to F, X, H, B; F connects to X and K; X connects to K and G; K connects to F and X; H connects to G and I; I connects to H, C, P; C connects to B, O, N; O connects to C, N, Y, P; P connects to I, O, J; B connects to E and N; N connects to B, C, O, Y.

Therefore, all L→J paths must pass through one of these two “gateways” from E:
1) E–M–J
2) E–G–…–P–J (since P is the only neighbor of J besides M and D, and D has no other connections).

Hence to disconnect L from J, it suffices to cut:
- the direct gateway via M: (M, J)
- the gateway into J from the lower network: any single edge on every E→P route. Observe E reaches P only through the subgraph starting at G; all E→P paths end by using edge (P, J), so cutting (P, J) blocks all of them simultaneously.

Verify minimality:
- Removing only (M, J) leaves paths L–E–G–…–P–J (e.g., L–E–G–H–I–P–J).
- Removing only (P, J) leaves path L–E–M–J.
- With both removed, J has no remaining connection from E’s side: the only remaining J edge would be to D, but D has no other links, so there is no path from L to J.

No smaller cut works (size 1 fails as shown). Thus the minimal cut size is 2.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(M, J), (P, J)]

  - Any path using P goes to I, then along the outer border to H (avoiding B and E entirely).

- Therefore, to disconnect O from H we must block:
  a) the “upper/center” routes that all funnel through either B or E, and
  b) the “lower/outer” route via P→I→…→H.

- Minimal cut:
  - Remove P to eliminate all paths that use the lower route O–P–I–…–H.
  - Remove either B or E to eliminate all remaining routes through the center/top.
  - With P removed and, say, B removed:
    • Paths via N or C cannot reach H because both required connectors (to H) are B or E, and B is gone; going via E requires B later to reach H, which is impossible.
    • Paths via P are already blocked.
  - Similarly, P and E is also sufficient.

- Check smaller cuts:
  - Any single node (B or E alone) leaves the P→I→…→H path intact.
  - Removing only P leaves multiple routes through N/C to B/E to H.
  - Hence at least 2 nodes are necessary.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [P, B] or [P, E]

   - [E, C]: Then H route still gives M→…→H→B. Not enough.
   - [E, N]: H route still exists. Not enough.
   - [H, C]: Top via E remains: M→E→B. Not enough.
   - [H, N]: Top via E remains. Not enough.
   - [C, N]: Top via E and M→…→E→B still exists. Not enough.

Thus any 2-node set fails. We need to remove three nodes to block all four attachments.

One minimal 3-node cut:
- Remove E to block the top route.
- Remove H to block the right/outer route.
- Remove either C or N to block the remaining middle-left entrance to B.

Verify:
- With nodes removed [E, H, C]:
   - Paths via E are impossible (E missing).
   - Paths via H are impossible (H missing).
   - Any left/middle route must use N→B (since C is gone), but can M reach N without H or E? Yes, via J–P–O–Y–N. However reaching B from N requires N–B, which still exists, so check: Does any alternate reach B avoiding removed nodes? Yes, N–B is still there; but that would reconnect. Therefore we must instead remove the other middle neighbor N (not C).

Try [E, H, N]:
- E removed: no top route.
- H removed: no right route.
- N removed: in the middle-left area, the only remaining attachment to B is C–B. But can M reach C and then B? Yes, J–P–I–C–B would use C–B. So we must remove C instead of N.

Hence to sever both middle attachments, we actually need to remove both C and N, in addition to either E or H. But that would be 4 nodes; check whether we can avoid removing H or E by cutting both C and N only:
- Remove [C, N]: Top E route remains, so need also E or H. So minimum may be 3 with [E, C, N] (cut both middle entrances and the top; right via H would still connect? H connects directly to B from M only if the outer path from M to H exists; it does via the outer frame. But even if H exists, without E and with both C and N removed, the only remaining neighbor of B is H. Since H is still present, M→outer→H→B would still connect. So we must remove H as well. That suggests 4 nodes.)

Re-evaluate: Are E and H both necessary? Is the outer frame from M to H uninterrupted without passing through removed nodes? Yes: M down the left border to bottom, along bottom to right, up to H. No removed nodes on borders. So H must be removed unless the edge H–B is somehow broken by removing another node (not possible).

Similarly, E–B is a direct edge; unless we remove E, that path exists.

Therefore, any valid cut must include both E and H. After removing E and H, we must also break all remaining connections from M region to B via C or N. But even if one of them remains, M can reach it and then directly to B. Therefore we must remove at least one of {C, N} in addition to E and H. That totals 3 nodes.

Choose [E, H, C] and re-check earlier worry:
- With E and H removed, does a path M→…→N→B exist? Yes, since N still connects to B. Can M reach N without going through E or H? M → J → P → O → Y → N is valid; none of those are removed. Then N → B gives a path. That means [E, H, C] fails.

Try [E, H, N]:
- M → … → C → B might remain: M → J → P → I → C → B. That’s a valid path, so [E, H, N] fails.

Therefore we must remove E, H, and also both C and N. Thus minimum is 4.

Check if any alternative avoids removing both C and N by cutting earlier articulation in the left subgraph so M cannot reach either C or N:
- If we remove P, then from M via left/bottom cannot get to I/O/Y region? But M can still go top to L→E (but E would be removed) or outer to H (H removed). To reach N or C without P: M could go M→J (left) but J to the central region requires P; also via outer frame up to H then to right box then to central? From H there is an edge to G then to K→X→F→E to B; but E and H removed; also to central via B? That would require B then to N/C, but path must end at B. The aim is to prevent reaching N or C from M. If we remove P instead of removing both C and N, can M reach N or C another way? From M to central without P: There is a vertical from M down left to bottom and then up to I (outer frame goes up right edge to H then left to B etc., not to I). The only connections from outer frame into the middle-left region are at I (bottom of vertical from C) via a vertical from I to bottom border? There’s a vertical from I to bottom border enclosing; the bottom border from left passes through I? The drawing shows I is on the bottom central line from J–P–I–(to right border). So without P, the segment J–I is broken; but M could go around outer frame to the right and back to I via the bottom edge? The bottom outer border goes straight; the inner bottom line passes through I and P and J, but the outer frame is a separate rectangle around; does it touch I? The inner bottom line seems to be the same as the outer bottom edge from left to right, connecting through I and P and J; and M’s left vertical meets that bottom line at J. Without P, can M reach I from J along the bottom? No, because the line J–P–I is continuous; removing P deletes that node, but edges J–P and P–I are removed. There is no bypass along the outer frame that skips P, since the continuous bottom line goes through P. So deleting P would cut M’s access to both I and further to C/N/Y/O.

Similarly removing O could block access to N and C from the left side, but M could reach C via I still if P remains.

- E removed: top gone.
- H removed: right/outer route gone.
- P removed: breaks the only conduit from M’s left side (J and outer left) to the central strip J–P–I and J–P–O. Without P, M cannot reach I, O, C, N, Y. Therefore no path to B via C or N remains.

The only visible entries from the left side are at J on the bottom line, which goes J–P–I; and the vertical from M to E at top. We’ve removed P and E, so M cannot reach I/C/N. Therefore no remaining path to B.

Minimality:
- Any cut must include E and H (because they are direct neighbors of B with independent access from M via top and outer border). If either E or H remained, a path would exist regardless of other removals.

- Via H: G–H–I is a direct path, so H alone connects them.
- Via E: E connects to M and B and F; from there to J/P/C/O/N/Y etc., and to I via J or P or H/C/O routes. So E provides routes to I.

- Via X: X connects to F, K, and G; from X→K→J→I or X→F→…→I, so X can reach I.

- Remove {H, E, F}; path G→X→K→J→I remains.
- Remove {H, E, X}; path G→F→K→J→I remains.
- Remove {H, F, X}; path G→E→…→J→I remains.
- Remove {E, F, X}; direct path G→H→I remains.

Thus any three are insufficient.

Try removing any single or pair:
- If H not removed, G–H–I exists.
- If H removed, need to block E, F, X simultaneously because G can go through any one of them to reach I via the network. Any pair among {E, F, X} removed leaves the third as a working route.

Therefore the minimum cut is size 4: remove all four neighbors of G.
